<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For the 2022 film, see <a href="/wiki/Padre_Pio_(film)" title="Padre Pio (film)">Padre Pio (film)</a>.</div>

<p><b>Francesco Forgione</b>, <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">OFMCap</a>, better known as <b>Padre Pio</b> and as <b>Saint Pius of Pietrelcina</b> (<a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Pio da Pietrelcina</i>; 25 May 1887&#160;&#8211;&#32;23 September 1968), was an Italian <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">Franciscan Capuchin</a> friar, <a href="/wiki/Priest" title="Priest">priest</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stigmatist" class="mw-redirect" title="Stigmatist">stigmatist</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Christian_mysticism" title="Christian mysticism">mystic</a>. He is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, celebrated on 23 September.
</p><p>Padre Pio became famous for exhibiting stigmata for most of his life, thereby generating much interest and controversy. He was both beatified in 1999 and canonized in 2002 by Pope <a href="/wiki/John_Paul_II." class="mw-redirect" title="John Paul II.">John Paul II.</a> The Sanctuary of Saint Pio of Pietrelcina is located in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a>, Province of Foggia, Italy.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Francesco Forgione was born to Grazio Mario Forgione (1860–1946) and Maria Giuseppa Di Nunzio (1859–1929) on May 25, 1887, in <a href="/wiki/Pietrelcina" title="Pietrelcina">Pietrelcina</a>, a town in the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Benevento" title="Province of Benevento">province of Benevento</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_Italy" title="Southern Italy">Southern Italian</a> region of <a href="/wiki/Campania" title="Campania">Campania</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> His parents were peasant farmers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerhold2007_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerhold2007-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He was baptized in the nearby Santa Anna Chapel, which stands upon the walls of a castle.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He later served as an <a href="/wiki/Altar_boy" class="mw-redirect" title="Altar boy">altar boy</a> in this same chapel. He had an older brother, Michele, and three younger sisters, Felicita, Pellegrina, and Grazia (who was later to become a <a href="/wiki/Bridgettines" title="Bridgettines">Bridgettine</a> nun).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGerhold2007_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerhold2007-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> His parents had two other children who died in infancy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> When he was baptized, he was given the name Francesco. He stated that by the time he was five years old, he had already made the decision to dedicate his entire life to God.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He worked on the land up to the age of 10, looking after the small flock of sheep the family owned.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Pietrelcina was a town where feast days of saints were celebrated throughout the year, and the Forgione family was deeply religious. They attended <a href="/wiki/Holy_Mass" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Mass">Mass</a> daily, prayed the <a href="/wiki/Rosary" title="Rosary">Rosary</a> nightly, and abstained from meat three days a week in honor of <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Mount_Carmel" title="Our Lady of Mount Carmel">Our Lady of Mount Carmel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Although Francesco's parents and grandparents were illiterate, they narrated Bible stories to their children.
</p><p>According to the diary of Father Agostino da San Marco (who was later his spiritual director in <a href="/wiki/San_Marco_in_Lamis" title="San Marco in Lamis">San Marco in Lamis</a>) the young Francesco was afflicted with a number of illnesses. At six he suffered from severe <a href="/wiki/Gastroenteritis" title="Gastroenteritis">gastroenteritis</a>. At ten he caught <a href="/wiki/Typhoid_fever" title="Typhoid fever">typhoid fever</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:KOS_O_PIO.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/KOS_O_PIO.jpg/220px-KOS_O_PIO.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/KOS_O_PIO.jpg/330px-KOS_O_PIO.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/KOS_O_PIO.jpg/440px-KOS_O_PIO.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1500" data-file-height="1000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:KOS_O_PIO.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The conventual cell of Padre Pio</div></div></div>
<p>As a youth, Francesco reported that he had experienced heavenly <a href="/wiki/Vision_(spirituality)" title="Vision (spirituality)">visions</a> and <a href="/wiki/Religious_ecstasy" title="Religious ecstasy">ecstasies</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In 1897, after he had completed three years at the public school, Francesco was said to have been drawn to the life of a friar after listening to a young <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Friars_Minor_Capuchin" title="Order of Friars Minor Capuchin">Capuchin</a> who was in the countryside seeking donations. When Francesco expressed his desire to his parents, they made a trip to <a href="/wiki/Morcone" title="Morcone">Morcone</a>, a community 13 miles (21&#160;km) north of Pietrelcina, to find out if their son was eligible to enter the Order. The friars there informed them that they were interested in accepting Francesco into their community, but he needed to be better educated.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Francesco's father went to the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> in search of work to pay for private tutoring for his son, to meet the academic requirements to enter the Capuchin Order.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> It was in this period that Francesco received the <a href="/wiki/Sacrament" title="Sacrament">sacrament</a> of <a href="/wiki/Confirmation" title="Confirmation">Confirmation</a> on 27 September 1899. He underwent private tutoring and passed the stipulated academic requirements. On 6 January 1903, at the age of 15, he entered the <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novitiate</a> of the Capuchin friars at Morcone. On 22 January, he took the <a href="/wiki/Franciscan" class="mw-redirect" title="Franciscan">Franciscan</a> <a href="/wiki/Religious_habit" title="Religious habit">habit</a> and the name of Fra (<a href="/wiki/Friar" title="Friar">Friar</a>) Pio, in honor of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_I" title="Pope Pius I">Pope Pius I</a>, whose <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">relic</a> is preserved in the Santa Anna Chapel in Pietrelcina.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-santanna_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-santanna-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> He took the <a href="/wiki/Religious_vows" title="Religious vows">simple vows</a> of <a href="/wiki/Poverty,_chastity_and_obedience" class="mw-redirect" title="Poverty, chastity and obedience">poverty, chastity and obedience</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991444-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Priesthood">Priesthood</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Priesthood">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:PIC_023.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/PIC_023.jpg/220px-PIC_023.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/PIC_023.jpg/330px-PIC_023.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/PIC_023.jpg/440px-PIC_023.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1600" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:PIC_023.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The church-shrine in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a>, Padre Pio's own church</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:S.Giovanni_Rotondo_(3)-2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg/220px-S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg/330px-S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg/440px-S.Giovanni_Rotondo_%283%29-2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:S.Giovanni_Rotondo_(3)-2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The altar of Padre Pio's church in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a></div></div></div>
<p>Commencing his seven-year study for the priesthood, Fra Pio travelled to the friary of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Francis_of_Assisi" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Francis of Assisi">Saint Francis of Assisi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Umbria" title="Umbria">Umbria</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn1_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn1-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> At 17, he fell ill, complaining of loss of appetite, insomnia, exhaustion, fainting spells, and <a href="/wiki/Migraines" class="mw-redirect" title="Migraines">migraines</a>. He vomited frequently and could digest only milk and cheese. Religious devotees point to this time as being that at which inexplicable phenomena allegedly began to occur. During prayers for example, Pio appeared to others to be in a stupor, as if he were absent. One of Pio's fellow friars later claimed to have seen him in <a href="/wiki/Religious_ecstasy" title="Religious ecstasy">
ecstasy</a>, and allegedly <a href="/wiki/Levitation_(paranormal)" title="Levitation (paranormal)">levitating</a> above the ground.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllegri199321_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllegri199321-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1905, Pio's health worsened to such an extent that his superiors decided to send him to a mountain convent, in the hope that the change of air would do him good. This had little impact, however, and doctors advised that he return home. Even there his health failed to improve. Despite this, he still made his solemn <a href="/wiki/Profession_(religious)" class="mw-redirect" title="Profession (religious)">profession</a> on 27 January 1907.
</p><p>In 1910, Pio was <a href="/wiki/Ordination" title="Ordination">ordained</a> a priest by <a href="/wiki/Archbishop" title="Archbishop">Archbishop</a> Paolo Schinosi at the Cathedral of <a href="/wiki/Benevento" title="Benevento">Benevento</a>. Four days later, he offered his first Mass at the parish church of <a href="/wiki/Blessed_Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Blessed Virgin Mary">Our Lady of the Angels</a>. His health being precarious, he was permitted to remain with his family until 1916 while still retaining the Capuchin habit.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 4 September 1916, however, Pio was ordered to return to his community life. He moved to an agricultural community, <a href="/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Grace" title="Our Lady of Grace">Our Lady of Grace</a> Capuchin <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">Friary</a>, located in the Gargano Mountains in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Province_of_Foggia" title="Province of Foggia">Province of Foggia</a>. At that time the community numbered seven friars. He remained at San Giovanni Rotondo until his death in 1968, except for a period of military service. In the priesthood, Padre Pio was known to perform a number of successful conversions to Catholicism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECarty1994128_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarty1994128-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pio was devoted to <a href="/wiki/Rosary_devotions_and_spirituality" title="Rosary devotions and spirituality">rosary meditations</a>. He compared weekly confession to dusting a room weekly, and recommended the performance of meditation and self-examination twice daily: once in the morning, as preparation to face the day, and once again in the evening, as retrospection. His advice on the practical application of theology he often summed up in his now famous quote: "Pray, Hope, and Don’t Worry". He directed Christians to recognize God in all things and to desire above all things to do the will of God.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn2_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn2-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many people who heard of him traveled to <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a> to meet him and confess to him, ask for help, or have their curiosity satisfied. Pio's mother died at the village around the convent in 1928. Later, in 1938, Pio had his elderly father Grazio live with him. His brother Michele also moved in. Pio's father lived in a little house outside the convent, until his death in 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESjöblom200310,_85_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESjöblom200310,_85-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_World_War_and_aftermath">First World War and aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: First World War and aftermath">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a> started, four friars from this community were selected for military service in the Italian army. At that time, Padre Pio was a teacher at the seminary and a spiritual director. When one more friar was called into service, Pio was put in charge of the community. On 15 November 1915, he was drafted and on December 6, assigned to the 10th Medical Corps in <a href="/wiki/Naples" title="Naples">Naples</a>. Due to poor health, he was continually discharged and recalled until on 16 March 1918, he was declared unfit for service and discharged completely.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>People who had started rebuilding their lives after the war began to see in Padre Pio a symbol of hope.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn2_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn2-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Those close to him attest that he began to manifest several spiritual gifts, including the gifts of healing, <a href="/wiki/Bilocation" title="Bilocation">bilocation</a>, levitation, <a href="/wiki/Prophecy" title="Prophecy">prophecy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Miracle" title="Miracle">miracles</a>, extraordinary abstinence from both sleep and nourishment (one account states that Padre Agostino recorded one instance in which Padre Pio was able to subsist for at least 20 days at Verafeno on only the Holy Eucharist without any other nourishment), the ability to read hearts, the gift of <a href="/wiki/Glossolalia" class="mw-redirect" title="Glossolalia">tongues</a>, the gift of conversions, and pleasant-smelling wounds.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPelletier2008_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelletier2008-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Padre Pio increasingly became well known among the wider populace. He became a spiritual director, and developed five rules for spiritual growth: weekly confession, daily Communion, spiritual reading, meditation, and examination of conscience.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn2_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn2-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_and_economic_activities">Political and economic activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Political and economic activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Padre Pio was not especially concerned with politics, but voted in Italian elections and voiced his opinions on various issues. He initially felt that <a href="/wiki/Benito_Mussolini" title="Benito Mussolini">Benito Mussolini</a> had done a good job during <a href="/wiki/Fascist_Italy_(1922%E2%80%931943)" title="Fascist Italy (1922–1943)">his rule</a>, but his feelings on Mussolini quickly became negative as time passed. When visited by one of Mussolini's messengers, Pio yelled at the man, "So now you come to me, after you have destroyed Italy. You can tell Mussolini that nothing can save Italy now! Nothing!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991225_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991225-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Pio also thought highly of the U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a>, who he described as a "great man."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, Pio expressed great concern over the spread of <a href="/wiki/Communism" title="Communism">communism</a> during his life and frequently prayed to help combat it.
</p><p>In August 1920, Pio led the blessing of a flag for a group of local veterans on the feast of the Assumption, and who were developing close links to local <a href="/wiki/Fascists" class="mw-redirect" title="Fascists">fascists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201168_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201168-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Pio subsequently met with <a href="/w/index.php?title=Giuseppe_Caradonna&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Giuseppe Caradonna (page does not exist)">Giuseppe Caradonna</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://it.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giuseppe_Caradonna" class="extiw" title="it:Giuseppe Caradonna">it</a>&#93;</span>, a fascist politician from <a href="/wiki/Foggia" title="Foggia">Foggia</a>, and became his confessor and that of members of his militia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201185,_89_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201185,_89-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Italian historian Sergio Luzzatto suggests in his book about Pio that Caradonna mounted a "praetorian guard" around Pio  to prevent any attempts to remove Padre Pio from the monastery and transfer him elsewhere.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201185_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201185-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> An early biographer of Pio, Emanuele Brunatto, also mediated between Pio and the leaders of the growing <a href="/wiki/Italian_Fascism" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian Fascism">Italian Fascist movement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011148_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011148-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>  Brunatto's publisher, Giorgio Berlutti, took part in the <a href="/wiki/March_on_Rome" title="March on Rome">March on Rome</a>, and used two Pio's biographies to promote the figure of Pio.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011149_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011149-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> It has been suggested that "a <a href="/wiki/Clerical_fascism" title="Clerical fascism">clerical-fascist</a> mixture developed around Padre Pio".<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Pio was a clear supporter of Mussolini very early and, according to Brunatto, said, "[Mussolini] is toiling for posterity. We pray to God because his life is in danger and the Lord would not want him go missing just now."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011158–159_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011158–159-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Brunatto was possibly active as a spy in France from 1931, but at the latest from 1935, on behalf of the fascist government and financed by it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._194_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._194-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Pio was connected to his biographer Brunatto in several ways: the latter acted as managing director of a joint-stock company called "Anonima Brevetti Zarlatti", which sold patents for diesel locomotives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._192_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._192-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> Luzzatto proved, based on Pio's handwritten notes, that the latter was directly involved in business transactions of the "Anonima Brevetti Zarlatti".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._192_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._192-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The shareholders bought corresponding shares, trusting in Pio's participation. After the company went bankrupt, the shareholders had the corresponding damage. The historian Luzzatto calls Brunatto a "chronic liar" and deceiver who brought together followers of Padre Pio with persons from the fascist hierarchy and likewise with leading persons from the Vatican.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._148_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._148-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1948, in a letter written to <a href="/wiki/Alcide_De_Gasperi" title="Alcide De Gasperi">Alcide De Gasperi</a>, Pio noted his support for the <a href="/wiki/Christian_Democracy_(Italy)" title="Christian Democracy (Italy)">Christian Democracy</a> party.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Pio's involvement is attributed to having helped the party win elections, with Italian communists hating Pio for it. One communist spokesman grumbled that Pio's presence at the voting polls "took votes away from us."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235_14-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991235-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the Christian Democracy's political victories in elections, Pio was continually consulted by political Italian leaders including <a href="/wiki/Aldo_Moro" title="Aldo Moro">Aldo Moro</a>, <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Segni" title="Antonio Segni">Antonio Segni</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mariano_Rumor" title="Mariano Rumor">Mariano Rumor</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Leone" title="Giovanni Leone">Giovanni Leone</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991276_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991276-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Pio received letters requesting his prayers throughout his life, including one from <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_XIII" title="Alfonso XIII">Alfonso XIII</a> in March 1923.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991184_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991184-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Pio also prayed for various notable political figures, including <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a>.
</p><p>In 1963, following the <a href="/wiki/Assassination_of_John_F._Kennedy" title="Assassination of John F. Kennedy">assassination of John F. Kennedy</a>, Pio broke down in tears. When asked by another priest if he would pray for Kennedy's salvation, Pio replied "It's not necessary. He's already in Paradise."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991294_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991294-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="La_Casa_Sollievo_della_Sofferenza_hospital">La Casa Sollievo della Sofferenza hospital</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: La Casa Sollievo della Sofferenza hospital">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg/220px-KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg/330px-KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/03/KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg/440px-KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1500" data-file-height="1000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:KOS_San_Giovanni_Rotondo.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The hospital that was built on Padre Pio's initiative in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>. (<i>Casa Sollievo della Sofferenza</i>)</div></div></div> <div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg/220px-Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="130" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg/330px-Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg/440px-Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2989" data-file-height="1772" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Benedict_Medal.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Saint_Benedict_Medal" title="Saint Benedict Medal">Saint Benedict Medal</a> was a gift Padre Pio often gave as a present to his visitors.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESjöblom2003_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESjöblom2003-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>By 1925, Pio had converted an old convent building into a medical clinic with a few beds intended primarily for people in extreme need.<sup id="cite_ref-cna_2018_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cna_2018-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> In 1940, a committee was set-up to establish a bigger clinic<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli2011293_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli2011293-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> and donations started to be made. Construction began in 1947.<sup id="cite_ref-cna_2018_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cna_2018-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Luzzatto, the bulk of the money for financing the hospital came directly from Emanuele Brunatto, a keen follower of Pio, who had made his fortune in the <a href="/wiki/Black_market" title="Black market">black market</a> in <a href="/wiki/German-occupied_France" class="mw-redirect" title="German-occupied France">German-occupied France</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011156,_205,_219_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011156,_205,_219-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Relief_and_Rehabilitation_Administration" title="United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Administration">United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Administration</a> (UNRRA) also contributed funding - 250 million Italian lire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011219_ff_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011219_ff-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lodovico Montini, head of <a href="/wiki/Democrazia_Cristiana" class="mw-redirect" title="Democrazia Cristiana">Democrazia Cristiana</a>, and his brother <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_Battista_Montini" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni Battista Montini">Giovanni Battista Montini</a> (later Pope Paul VI) facilitated engagement by UNRRA.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011221_f_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011221_f-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The hospital was initially to be named "<a href="/wiki/Fiorello_LaGuardia" class="mw-redirect" title="Fiorello LaGuardia">Fiorello LaGuardia</a>", but eventually presented as the work of Pio himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011225_ff.,_228_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011225_ff.,_228-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The <span title="Italian-language text"><span lang="it" style="font-style: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Casa_Sollievo_della_Sofferenza" title="Casa Sollievo della Sofferenza">Casa Sollievo della Sofferenza</a></span></span> (English: <i lang="en">Home for Relief of Suffering</i>) opened in 1956.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli2011293_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli2011293-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Pio handed direct control to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>. However, in order that Pio might directly supervise the project, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> granted him a dispensation from his vow of poverty in 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-time2_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time2-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-clairval_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-clairval-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Some of Pio's detractors have subsequently suggested there had been misappropriation of funds.<sup id="cite_ref-time2_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time2-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Pio.27s_views"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Pio's_views">Pio's views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Pio&#039;s views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pio was a strong proponent of weekly confession, describing it as "the soul's bath." Pio established five rules for spiritual growth, which included weekly confession, daily communion, spiritual reading, meditation, and frequent examination of one's conscience."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991125_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991125-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> He taught his spiritual followers that suffering is a special sign of God's love, for it makes you "resemble His divine son in His <a href="/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ" title="Temptation of Christ">anguish in the desert</a> and on the <a href="/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus" title="Crucifixion of Jesus">hill of Calvary</a>."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991128_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991128-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pio held a harsh attitude towards vain women, stating: "Women who satisfy their vanity in their dress can never put on the life of Jesus Christ; moreover they even lose the ornaments of their soul as soon as this idol enters into their heart."<sup id="cite_ref-SSPX_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SSPX-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pio also held to strict rules concerning modesty, and refused confession to women who did not wear skirts that extended a minimum of 8 inches (20&#160;cm) past the knees.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He posted a notice at the entrance of the Church of St. Mary of All Graces in <a href="/wiki/San_Giovanni_Rotondo" title="San Giovanni Rotondo">San Giovanni Rotondo</a>, reading: "The Church is the house of God. It is forbidden for men to enter with bare arms or in shorts. It is forbidden for women to enter in trousers, without a veil on their head, in short clothing, low necklines, sleeveless or immodest dresses."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGunn2012102_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGunn2012102-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pio was deeply troubled by the changes the church was undergoing following the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a>. Pio accepted the changes the council brought, but immediately requested a dispensation to celebrate the <a href="/wiki/Tridentine_mass" class="mw-redirect" title="Tridentine mass">Tridentine mass</a>, which was granted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991296_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991296-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> When visited by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Bacci" title="Antonio Bacci">Antonio Bacci</a>, Pio let a small complaint slip in the Cardinal's presence: "For pity sake, end the Council quickly." In 1966, the Father General of the Franciscans came to Pio to ask for prayers regarding the reformation of the Franciscans. Upon hearing about the "new constitutions", Pio replied "That is all nothing but destructive nonsense."<sup id="cite_ref-SSPX_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SSPX-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, although he was distraught over the changes, he emphasized obedience to the church. On one occasion, Pio met with Suor Pia, a former nun who left her order following the council. Suor Pia was a traditionalist and was upset at the changes made by her liberal superiors, causing her to leave her convent at the age of seventy. Pio burst into tears and snapped at her over this decision, telling her "They are wrong and you are right, but you still must obey. You must return." She refused, causing him to weep uncontrollably and continue praying for her.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991297_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991297-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the publication of <i><a href="/wiki/Humanae_vitae" title="Humanae vitae">Humanae vitae</a></i>, Pio was distraught over <a href="/wiki/Humanae_vitae#Response_and_criticism" title="Humanae vitae">criticism aimed at the encyclical</a>. He wrote <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> over this, affirming his obedience to the Church's teaching on birth control and reassuring Paul VI in his time of need. Pio informed the pope that he would offer up his daily prayers and suffering for the pontiff, due to Paul VI's defense of "eternal truth, which never changes with the passing of years."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991303_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991303-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Pio grew older, he became increasingly distrustful of television. After the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>, when Pio's nephew, Ettorne Masone, asked Pio for advice on opening a movie house, Pio warned him to be careful about what movies he would show. Pio stated "You don't want to contribute to the propagation of evil."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991234_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991234-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> By the 1960s, Pio was displeased that the Capuchins were now permitted to watch television. To Pio, television was responsible for the destruction of the family life and he strongly warned others not to buy one when asked. On one occasion, when asked about motion pictures, Pio replied "The devil is in it!" On another occasion, Pio told a penitent in confession that the reason the penitent's car had broken down the day before was because the penitent was driving to a movie theater.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991295_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991295-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pio became exceedingly pessimistic about the state of the world towards the end of his life. When asked what awaited the world in the future, Pio replied "Can't you see the world is catching on fire?"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991296_43-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991296-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In his last three years, he began to withdraw further from life, feeling unworthy and unsure of his salvation. Pio frequently asked his superior, "Give me the obedience to die."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991300_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991300-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Investigations_by_medical_and_church_authorities">Investigations by medical and church authorities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Investigations by medical and church authorities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Vatican initially imposed severe sanctions on Pio in the 1920s to reduce publicity about him: it forbade him from saying Mass in public, blessing people, answering letters, showing his stigmata publicly, and communicating with Padre Benedetto, his spiritual director.
</p><p>The church authorities decided that Pio be relocated to another convent in northern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> 
The local people threatened to riot, and the Vatican left him where he was.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllen2001_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2001-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> A second plan for removal was also changed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllen2001_50-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2001-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Nevertheless, from 1921 to 1922 he was prevented from publicly performing his priestly duties, such as hearing confessions and saying Mass.<sup id="cite_ref-time1_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time1-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> From 1924 to 1931, the Holy See made statements denying that the events in Pio's life were due to any divine cause.<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn2_9-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn2-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Luigi_Romanelli.2C_medical_examination_from_1919"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Luigi_Romanelli,_medical_examination_from_1919">Luigi Romanelli, medical examination from 1919</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Luigi Romanelli, medical examination from 1919">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A large number of doctors visited Padre Pio to verify that he was not a braggart. The first to study his wounds was Luigi Romanelli, chief physician of the civil hospital of Barletta, by order of the provincial father superior, on May 15 and 16, 1919. In his report, among other things he wrote: "The injuries he presents to hands are covered with a brown-red membrane, with no bleeding points, no edema and no inflammatory reactions in the surrounding tissues. I am sure that those wounds are not superficial because, by applying the thumb in the palm of the hand and the index finger on the back and applying pressure, one has the exact perception of the existing void."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllegri199360–61_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllegri199360–61-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Amico_Bignami.2C_medical_examination_from_1919"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Amico_Bignami,_medical_examination_from_1919">Amico Bignami, medical examination from 1919</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Amico Bignami, medical examination from 1919">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Two months later, on July 26, pathologist Amico Bignami arrived in San Giovanni Rotondo. Bignami conducted a medical examination of Pio's wounds in 1919 and launched several hypotheses, among which was that the wounds were a <a href="/wiki/Skin" title="Skin">skin</a> <a href="/wiki/Necrosis" title="Necrosis">necrosis</a> that was hindered from healing by chemicals such as <a href="/wiki/Tincture_of_iodine" title="Tincture of iodine">iodine tincture</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201139_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201139-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Giorgio_Festa.2C_medical_examinations_1919_and_1920"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Giorgio_Festa,_medical_examinations_1919_and_1920">Giorgio Festa, medical examinations 1919 and 1920</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Giorgio Festa, medical examinations 1919 and 1920">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Festa was a physician who examined Pio in 1919 and 1920. He was obviously impressed by the fragrance of the stigmata.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011139_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011139-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Festa, as Bignami before, had described the side wound as cruciform.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_44,_49ff._55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_44,_49ff.-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In his report to the Holy Office of 1925, Festa arrived at a benevolent verdict and attacked Gemelli's critical view of Pio's stigmata, with theological arguments playing the lead role.
</p>
<h3><span id="Agostino_Gemelli.2C_psychiatric_examination_1920_and_medical_examination_1925"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Agostino_Gemelli,_psychiatric_examination_1920_and_medical_examination_1925">Agostino Gemelli, psychiatric examination 1920 and medical examination 1925</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Agostino Gemelli, psychiatric examination 1920 and medical examination 1925">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1920, father <a href="/wiki/Agostino_Gemelli" title="Agostino Gemelli">Agostino Gemelli</a> – a physician and psychologist – was commissioned by Cardinal Rafael Merry del Val to visit Padre Pio and carry out a clinical examination of the wounds. "For this reason, despite having gone to Gargano Peninsula on his own initiative, without being asked by any ecclesiastical authority, Gemelli did not hesitate to make his private letter to the Holy Office a kind of unofficial report on Padre Pio."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201143_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201143-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Gemelli wanted to express himself fully on the matter and wanted to meet the friar. Padre Pio showed a closed attitude towards the new investigator: he refused the visit requesting the written authorization of the Holy Office. Father Gemelli's protests that he believed he had the right to subject the friar to a medical examination of the stigmata were in vain. The friar, supported by his superiors, conditioned the examination to a permit requested through the hierarchy, without taking into account the credentials of Father Agostino Gemelli. Therefore, Gemelli left the convent, irritated and offended for not being allowed to examine the stigmata. He came to the conclusion that Francesco Forgione was "a man of restricted field of knowledge, low psychic energy, monotonous ideas, little volition."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201159_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201159-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Gemelli critically judged Pio: "The case is one of suggestion unconsciously planted by Father Benedetto in the weak mind of Padre Pio, producing those characteristic manifestations of <a href="/wiki/Psittacism" title="Psittacism">psittacism</a> that are intrinsic to the hysteric mind."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201159_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201159-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On behalf of the Holy Office, Gemelli re-examined Padre Pio in 1925, writing a report in April 1926. This time Pio allowed him to see the wounds. Gemelli saw as its cause the use of a corrosive substance Pio had attached himself to these wounds. The Jesuit Festa had previously tried to question Gemelli's comments on stigmata in general.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Gemelli responded to this criticism in his report and resorted to responding to his knowledge of self-inflicted wounds. He therefore clarified his statements about the nature of Pio's wounds: "Anyone with experience in forensic medicine, and above all in variety by sores and wounds that self-destructive soldiers were presented during the war, can have no doubt that these were wounds of erosion caused by the use of a caustic substance. The base of the sore and its shape are in every way similar to the sores observed in soldiers who procured them with chemical means."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Once again, Gemelli judged Padre Pio's mental abilities as limited: "He [Pio] is the ideal partner with whom former Minister Provincial Father Benedetto is able to create an incubus-succubus pair ... He is a good priest: calm, quiet, meek, more because of the mental deficiency than out of virtue. A poor soul, able to repeat a few stereotypical religious phrases, a poor, sick man who has learned his lesson from his master, Father Benedetto."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011141_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011141-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Gemelli wrote in 1940 and later several times to the Holy Office on what he considered to be unjustified claims to the sanctity of Padre Pio.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011259_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011259-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Raffaele_Rossi.2C_First_Apostolic_Visitation_of_1921"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Raffaele_Rossi,_First_Apostolic_Visitation_of_1921">Raffaele Rossi, First Apostolic Visitation of 1921</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Raffaele Rossi, First Apostolic Visitation of 1921">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Bishop of Volterra, <a href="/wiki/Raffaele_Carlo_Rossi" class="mw-redirect" title="Raffaele Carlo Rossi">Raffaele Rossi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Discalced_Carmelite" class="mw-redirect" title="Discalced Carmelite">Carmelite</a>, was formally commissioned on June 11, 1921 by the <a href="/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith" class="mw-redirect" title="Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith">Holy Office</a> to make a canonical inquiry concerning Father Pio. Rossi began his <a href="/wiki/Canonical_visitation" title="Canonical visitation">Apostolic Visitation</a> on June 14 in San Giovanni Rotondo with the interrogation of witnesses, two <a href="/wiki/Diocesan_priests" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocesan priests">diocesan priests</a> and seven <a href="/wiki/Friars" class="mw-redirect" title="Friars">friars</a>. After eight days of investigation, he finally completed a benevolent report, which he sent to the Holy Office on October 4, <span class="nowrap">1921&#8202;—</span><span style="white-space: nowrap;">&#8201;</span>the feast of St. Francis of Assisi. The extensive and detailed report essentially stated the following: Father Pio, of whom Rossi had a favorable impression, was a good religious and the San Giovanni Rotondo convent was a good community. The stigmata cannot be explained, but certainly are not a work of the devil or an act of gross deceit or fraud; neither are they the trick of a devious and malicious person.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_28,_67_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_28,_67-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>During the interviews with the witnesses, which Rossi undertook a total of three times, he let himself be shown the stigmata of the then 34-year-old Father Pio. Rossi saw these stigmata as a “real fact”.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201121_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201121-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his notes, which have been put directly on paper, and the final report, Rossi describes the shape and appearance of the wounds. Those in the hands were "very visible". Those in the feet "were disappearing. What could be observed resembled two dot-shaped elevations<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> with whiter and gentler skin."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201121_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201121-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> As for the chest, it says: "In his side, the sign is represented by a triangular spot, the color of red wine, and by other smaller ones, not anymore, then, by a sort of upside-down cross such as the one seen in 1919 by Dr. Bignami and Dr. Festa."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201122f_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201122f-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Rossi also made a request to the Holy Office, a chronicle to consult with Father Pio, who is assembling Father Benedetto, or at least to have the material he has collected so that one day one can write about the life of Father Pio.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201128_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201128-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Rossi "Of the alleged healings, many are unconfirmed or non-existent. In Padre Pio’s correspondence, however, there are some credible declarations that attribute miracles to his intercession. But without medical confirmation it is difficult to reach a conclusion, and the issue remains open."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_100ff.,_139ff._66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_100ff.,_139ff.-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/w/index.php?title=Lucia_Ceci&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Lucia Ceci (page does not exist)">Lucia Ceci</a>, Rossi could not find any of the attributed miracles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECeci2016114_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECeci2016114-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Rossi asked him about bilocation, Pio replied: "I don’t know how it is or the nature of this phenomenon—and I certainly don’t give it much thought—but it did happen to me to be in the presence of this or that person, to be in this or that place; I do not know whether my mind was transported there, or what I saw was some sort of representation of the place or the person; I do not know whether I was there with my body or without it.".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011102_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011102-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201132_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201132-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="John_XXIII.2C_investigations_and_tape_recordings.2C_after_1958"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="John_XXIII,_investigations_and_tape_recordings,_after_1958">John XXIII, investigations and tape recordings, after 1958</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: John XXIII, investigations and tape recordings, after 1958">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pope <a href="/wiki/John_XXIII" class="mw-redirect" title="John XXIII">John XXIII</a> was skeptical of Padre Pio. At the beginning of his tenure, he learned that Father Pio's opponents had placed listening devices in his monastery cell and confessional, recording his confessions with tape.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011270_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011270-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Outside his semi-official journal, John XXIII wrote on four sheets of paper that he prayed for "PP" (Padre Pio) and the discovery by means of tapes, if what they imply is true, of his intimate and indecent relationships with women from his impenetrable praetorian guard around his person pointed to a terrible calamity of souls.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011270_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011270-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> John XXIII had probably never listened to the tapes himself, but assumed the correctness of this view: "The reason for my spiritual tranquillity, and it is a priceless privilege and grace, is that I feel personally pure of this contamination that for forty years has corroded hundreds of thousands of souls made foolish and deranged to an unheard-of degree."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._271_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._271-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> According to Luzzatto, the Vatican had not ordered this wiretap. In another journal note, John XXIII wrote that he wanted to take action. In fact, he ordered another Apostolic Visitation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._271_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._271-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Carlo_Maccari.2C_Second_Apostolic_Visitation_of_1960"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Carlo_Maccari,_Second_Apostolic_Visitation_of_1960">Carlo Maccari, Second Apostolic Visitation of 1960</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Carlo Maccari, Second Apostolic Visitation of 1960">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Father Carlo Maccari was Secretary-General of the Diocese of Rome and met Pio nine times altogether.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011272_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011272-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> There was reciprocal mistrust between Pio and Maccari, who wrote in his diary: "Reticence, narrowness of mind, lies - these are the weapons he uses to evade my questions ... Overall impression: pitiful."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011273_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011273-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Maccari demanded Father Pio's omission to practice kisses after the confession for the lay sisters. Maccari noted in his report that Pio had inadequate religious education. He works a lot for a man of his age. He is not an <a href="/wiki/Asceticism" title="Asceticism">ascetic</a> and has many connections to the outside world. In general, there is too much mixing of the "sacred" and the "all too human".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011274_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011274-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> In his report, Maccari noted by name the women who revealed at which time to have been the lover of Pio, but without assessing the veracity of these statements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011274_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011274-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Maccari focused on assessing the fanaticism of Pio's social environment, describing it as "religious conceptions that oscillate between superstition and magic."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011275_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011275-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Maccari called Pio's supporters "a vast and dangerous organization."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011276_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011276-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> Pio never had his own supporters advised to moderation. Maccari wondered how God could allow "so much deception."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011277_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011277-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Maccari finished his critical report with a list of recommendations for further dealing with Father Pio. The brothers of Santa Maria delle Grazie should gradually be relocated, a new abbot should come from outside the region. No one should be allowed to confess to Pio more than once a month. The hospital was to be given new statutes to sever the responsibilities of the medical and spiritual "healing" capuchins.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011277_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011277-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Following Maccari's Apostolic Visitation, John XXIII noted in his diary that he sees Father Pio as a "straw idol" (<i>idolo di stoppa</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011278_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011278-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Reported_supernatural_phenomena">Reported supernatural phenomena</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Reported supernatural phenomena">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pio was said to have had the gift of reading <a href="/wiki/Soul" title="Soul">souls</a> and the ability to <a href="/wiki/Bilocation" title="Bilocation">bilocate</a>, among other <a href="/wiki/Supernatural" title="Supernatural">supernatural</a> phenomena. He was said to communicate with <a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">angels</a> and work favors and <a href="/wiki/Faith_healing" title="Faith healing">healings</a> before they were requested of him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECruz1997581_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECruz1997581-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> The reports of supernatural phenomena surrounding Padre Pio attracted fame and amazement. The Vatican was initially skeptical. According to the bishop of Volterra, Raffaele Rossi "Of the alleged healings, many are unconfirmed or non-existent. In Padre Pio’s correspondence, however, there are some credible declarations that attribute miracles to his intercession. But without medical confirmation it is difficult to reach a conclusion, and the issue remains open."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_100ff.,_139ff._66-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201120,_100ff.,_139ff.-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>When Rossi asked him about bilocation, Pio replied: "I don’t know how it is or the nature of this phenomenon—and I certainly don’t give it much thought—but it did happen to me to be in the presence of this or that person, to be in this or that place; I do not know whether my mind was transported there, or what I saw was some sort of representation of the place or the person; I do not know whether I was there with my body or without it."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011102_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011102-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201132_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201132-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Stigmata">Stigmata</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Stigmata">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pio wrote in his letters that, early in his priesthood, he experienced bodily marks, pain, and bleeding in locations indicative of the (not yet visible) <a href="/wiki/Stigmata" title="Stigmata">stigmata</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-stigmata_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stigmata-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> In a letter to his spiritual companion and confessor Father <a href="/wiki/Agostino_Gemelli" title="Agostino Gemelli">Agostino Gemelli</a>, dated March 21, 1912, Pio wrote of his devotion to the mystical body of Christ and the intuition that he would bear the stigmata. Luzzatto points out that in this letter Pio uses unrecognized passages from a book by the allegedly stigmatized mystic <a href="/wiki/Gemma_Galgani" title="Gemma Galgani">Gemma Galgani</a>. Pio subsequently denied that he knew of or owned the cited book.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201119_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201119-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a 1915 letter, Agostino asked Pio specific questions, including: when did he first experience visions, whether he was stigmatic, and whether he felt the pains of the <a href="/wiki/Passion_(Christianity)" class="mw-redirect" title="Passion (Christianity)">Passion of Christ</a>, namely the crowning of thorns and the scourging. Pio replied that he was having visions since his novitiate period (1903 to 1904), and that he was stigmatic, adding that he had been so terrified by the phenomenon that he begged God to withdraw his stigmata. He also wrote that he did not wish the pain to be removed, only the visible wounds, since he considered them to be an indescribable and almost unbearable humiliation.<sup id="cite_ref-stigmata_80-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stigmata-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 20 September 1918, while hearing confessions, Pio said to have had a reappearance of the physical occurrence of the stigmata. His stigmatism was reported to have continued for fifty years, until the end of his life. The blood flowing from the stigmata purportedly smelled of perfume or flowers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201155,_190–191_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201155,_190–191-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Pio conveyed to Agostino that the pain remained and was more acute on specific days and under certain circumstances. Though he said he would have preferred to suffer in secret, by early 1919, news that he was a stigmatic had begun to spread. Pio often wore red mittens or black coverings on his hands and feet, saying that he was embarrassed by the marks.<sup id="cite_ref-time2_36-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time2-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Agostino Gemelli claimed that the wounds were consistent with those that soldiers had <a href="/wiki/Self-inflicted_wound" title="Self-inflicted wound">inflicted on themselves</a> "by the use of a caustic substance".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011140-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/Amico_Bignami" title="Amico Bignami">Amico Bignami</a> considered that Pio's wounds might be a skin necrosis that was hindered from healing through the use of iodine tincture or similar chemicals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201139_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201139-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Padre-Pio-young.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/48/Padre-Pio-young.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="214" class="thumbimage" data-file-width="150" data-file-height="214" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Padre-Pio-young.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Padre Pio showing the <a href="/wiki/Stigmata" title="Stigmata">stigmata</a> (detail from a photo from August 19, 1919)<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Once made public, the wounds were studied by a number of physicians, some hired by the Vatican as part of an independent investigation. Some claimed that the wounds were unexplainable and never seem to have become infected.<sup id="cite_ref-time2_36-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time2-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreze1989283-285_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreze1989283-285-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Despite seeming to heal they would then reappear periodically.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Alberto Caserta  took <a href="/wiki/X-ray" title="X-ray">X-rays</a> of Pio's hands in 1954 and found no abnormality in the bone structure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuffin1991160–163_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuffin1991160–163-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> Some critics accused Pio of faking the stigmata, for example by using <a href="/wiki/Carbolic_acid" class="mw-redirect" title="Carbolic acid">carbolic acid</a> to make the wounds. Maria De Vito (the cousin of the local pharmacist Valentini Vista at <a href="/wiki/Foggia" title="Foggia">Foggia</a>) testified that the young Pio bought carbolic acid and the great quantity of four grams of <a href="/wiki/Veratridine" title="Veratridine">veratrine</a> "without presenting any medical prescription whatsoever" and "in great secret".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201191,_92_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto201191,_92-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Veratrine is a "mixture of alkaloids", a "highly caustic product": "Veratrine is so poisonous, that only a doctor can decide whether to prescribe it" as the pharmacist Vista stated in front of witnesses.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._91_ff_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011S._91_ff-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>  Veratrine was once used as a paralyzing muscle insecticide, primarily against lice, but was also described by pharmacists as an "external stimulant" that renders you insensitive to pain.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>  Pio maintained that the carbolic acid was used to sterilize syringes used for medical treatments and that after being subjected to a practical joke where veratrine was mixed with snuff tobacco, causing uncontrollable sneezing after ingestion, he decided to acquire his own quantity of the substance in order to play the same joke on his confreres;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011103_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELuzzatto2011103-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201125,_114_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201125,_114-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> the bishop of Volterra, <a href="/wiki/Raffaele_Rossi" title="Raffaele Rossi">Raffaello Rossi</a> came to share this view, believing that "Instead of malice, what is revealed here is Padre Pio's simplicity, and his playful spirit"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201125,_114_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201125,_114-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> and that "the stigmata at issue are not a work of the devil, nor a gross deceit, a fraud, the trick of a devious and malicious person [...] his "stigmata" do not seem to me a morbid product of external suggestion."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli2011124_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli2011124-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> Rossi saw these stigmata as a “real fact”.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECastelli201121_62-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECastelli201121-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Healing">Healing</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Healing">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the 1999 book <i>Padre Pio: The Wonder Worker</i>, a segment by Irish priest Malachy Gerard Carroll describes the story of Gemma de Giorgi, a Sicilian girl whose blindness was believed to have been cured during a visit to Padre Pio.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Gemma, who was brought to San Giovanni Rotondo in 1947 by her grandmother, was born without pupils. During her trip to see Padre Pio, the little girl began to see objects, including a steamboat and the sea.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210_93-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> Gemma's grandmother did not believe the child had been healed. After Gemma forgot to ask Padre Pio for <a href="/wiki/Actual_grace" class="mw-redirect" title="Actual grace">grace</a> during her confession, her grandmother implored the priest to ask God to restore her sight.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210_93-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> Padre Pio told her, "The child must not weep and neither must you for the child sees and you know she sees."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210_93-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalvelage1999210-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg/220px-Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="139" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg/330px-Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg/440px-Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1030" data-file-height="650" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Pio_of_Pietrelcina.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Padre Pio helped by other friars</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transverberation">Transverberation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Transverberation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At 5 and 7 August, Padre Pio claimed to have received a physical wound in his side. This occurrence is considered as a <a href="/wiki/Transverberation" class="mw-redirect" title="Transverberation">transverberation</a> or "piercing of the heart", indicating the union of love with God within Christian mysticism. 
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg/220px-3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="114" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg/330px-3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg/440px-3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3620" data-file-height="1869" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:3rd_class_relic_PIO.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Many books about Padre Pio included a <a href="/wiki/Relic" title="Relic">third-class relic</a> (cloth) on a prayer card. This relic was encased when he was considered "<a href="/wiki/Servant_of_God#Catholic_Church" title="Servant of God">Venerable</a>," but he has since been <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonized</a>.</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg/220px-Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg/330px-Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg/440px-Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2736" data-file-height="3648" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Monumento_a_Padre_Pio_04.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Sculpture of Padre Pio with Jesus on the cross in <a href="/wiki/Prato" title="Prato">Prato</a>, Italy</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Prophecy">Prophecy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Prophecy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1947, 27-year old Father Karol Józef Wojtyła (later <a href="/wiki/Pope_John_Paul_II" title="Pope John Paul II">Pope John Paul II</a>) visited Padre Pio, who heard his confession. Austrian <a href="/wiki/Alfons_Stickler" class="mw-redirect" title="Alfons Stickler">Cardinal Alfons Stickler</a> reported that Wojtyła confided to him that during this meeting, Padre Pio told him he would one day ascend to "the highest post in the church, though further confirmation is needed."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKwitny1997168_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKwitny1997168-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Stickler said that Wojtyła believed that the prophecy was fulfilled when he became a cardinal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZahn2002_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahn2002-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> John Paul's secretary, <a href="/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Dziwisz" title="Stanisław Dziwisz">Stanisław Dziwisz</a>, denies the prediction,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDziwisz2008_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDziwisz2008-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> while <a href="/wiki/George_Weigel" title="George Weigel">George Weigel's</a> biography <i>Witness to Hope</i>, which contains an account of the same visit, does not mention it.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2023)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Rehabilitation">Rehabilitation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Rehabilitation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1933, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI" title="Pope Pius XI">Pope Pius XI</a> ordered a reversal of the ban on Padre Pio's public celebration of Mass, arguing, "I have not been badly disposed toward Padre Pio, but I have been badly informed."<sup id="cite_ref-ewtn2_9-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ewtn2-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In 1934, the friar was again allowed to hear confessions. He was also given honorary permission to preach despite never having taken the exam for the preaching license. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a>, who assumed the papacy in 1939, even encouraged devotees to visit Padre Pio.
</p><p>Finally, in the mid-1960s <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI" title="Pope Paul VI">Pope Paul VI</a> (pope from 1963 to 1978) dismissed all accusations against Padre Pio.<sup id="cite_ref-time2_36-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-time2-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllen2001_50-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2001-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Padre_Pio&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
